| *(didascalia)* | Enter Peto |
| PRINCE HARRY | Peto, how now, what news? |
| PETO | The [[King]] your father is at Westminster;<br>And there are twenty weak and wearied posts<br>Come from the north; and as I came along<br>I met and overtook a dozen captains,<br>Bareheaded, sweating, knocking at the taverns,<br>And asking every one for Sir John [[Falstaff]]. |
| PRINCE HARRY | By heaven, Poins, I feel me much to blame<br>So idly to profane the precious [[Time\|time]],<br>When tempest of commotion, like the south<br>Borne with black vapour, doth begin to melt<br>And drop upon our bare unarmed heads.—<br>Give me my [[Sword\|sword]] and cloak.—[[Falstaff]], good [[Night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Prince Harry and Poins |
| SIR JOHN | Now comes in the sweetest morsel of the [[Night\|night]],<br>and we must hence and leave it unpicked. 371 |
| *(didascalia)* | Knocking within. Exit Bardolph |
| SIR JOHN | More knocking at the door! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph |
| SIR JOHN | How now, what’s the matter? |
| BARDOLPH | You must away to [[Court\|court]], sir, presently.<br>A dozen captains stay at door for you. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN to the Page Pay the musicians, sirrah. Farewell, hostess; farewell, Doll. You see, my good wenches, how men of merit are sought after. The undeserver may [[Sleep\|sleep]], when the man of action is called on. Farewell, good wenches. If I be not sent away post, I will see you again ere I go. Exeunt musicians |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] weeping I cannot speak. If my heart be not ready to burst—[[Well\|well]], sweet Jack, have a care of thyself. |
| SIR JOHN | Farewell, farewell! 385 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with Bardolph, Peto, and the Page |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | [[Well]], fare thee [[Well\|well]]. I have known thee these twenty-nine years come peascod-[[Time\|time]], but an honester and truer-hearted man—[[Well\|well]], fare thee [[Well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph |
| BARDOLPH | Mistress Tearsheet! |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | What’s the matter? 390 |
| BARDOLPH | Bid Mistress Tearsheet come to my master. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | O run, Doll; run, run, good Doll! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Doll at one door, [[Mistress Quickly]] at another door |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Henry in his nightgown, with a page |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (giving letters)<br>Go call the Earls of Surrey and of Warwick.<br>But ere they come, bid them o’er-read these letters<br>And [[Well\|well]] consider of them. Make good [[Speed\|speed]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit page |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | How many thousand of my poorest subjects<br>Are at this hour asleep? O [[Sleep\|sleep]], O gentle [[Sleep\|sleep]],<br>[[Nature]]’s soft [[Nurse\|nurse]], how have I frighted thee,<br>That thou no more wilt weigh my eyelids down<br>And steep my senses in forgetfulness?<br>Why rather, [[Sleep\|sleep]], liest thou in smoky cribs,<br>Upon uneasy pallets stretching thee,<br>And hushed with buzzing [[Night\|night]]-flies to thy slumber,<br>Than in the perfumed chambers of the great,<br>Under the canopies of costly state,<br>And lulled with sound of sweetest melody?<br>O thou dull god, why li‘st thou with the vile 15<br>In loathsome beds, and leav’st the kingly couch<br>A watch-case, or a common ’larum-bell?<br>Wilt thou upon the high and giddy mast<br>Seal up the [[Ship\|ship]]-boy’s eyes, and rock his brains<br>In cradle of the rude imperious surge,<br>And in the visitation of the winds,<br>Who take the ruffian billows by the top,<br>Curling their monstrous heads, and hanging them<br>With deafing clamour in the slippery clouds,<br>That, with the hurly, [[Death\|death]] itself awakes?<br>Canst thou, O partial [[Sleep\|sleep]], give thy repose<br>To the wet sea-boy in an hour so rude,<br>And in the calmest and most stillest [[Night\|night]],<br>With all appliances and means to boot,<br>Deny it to a [[King\|king]]? Then happy low, lie down.<br>Uneasy lies the head that wears a [[Crown\|crown]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earls of Warwick and Surrey |
| WARWICK | Many good morrows to your majesty! |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Is it good morrow, lords? |
| WARWICK | ’Tis one o’[[Clock\|clock]], and past. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why then, good morrow to you all, my lords.<br>Have you read o’er the letter that I sent you? |
| WARWICK | We have, my liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Then you perceive the [[Body\|body]] of our kingdom,<br>How foul it is, what rank diseases grow,<br>And with what danger near the heart of it. |
| WARWICK | It is but as a [[Body\|body]] yet distempered,<br>Which to his former strength may be restored<br>With good advice and little medicine.<br>My lord Northumberland will soon be cooled. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O God, that one might read the book of fate,<br>And see the revolution of the times<br>Make [[Mountains\|mountains]] level, and the continent,<br>Weary of solid firmness, melt itself<br>Into the sea; and other times to see<br>The beachy girdle of the ocean<br>Too wide for Neptune’s hips; how chance’s mocks<br>And changes fill the cup of alteration<br>With divers liquors!‘Tis not ten years gone<br>Since [[Richard]] and Northumberland, great friends,<br>Did feast together; and in two year after<br>Were they at wars. It is but eight years since<br>This Percy was the man nearest my [[Soul\|soul]],<br>Who like a brother toiled in my affairs,<br>And laid his [[Love\|love]] and life under my foot,<br>Yea, for my sake, even to the eyes of [[Richard]]<br>Gave him defiance. But which of you was by—<br>(To Warwick) You, cousin Neville, as I may<br>remember—<br>When [[Richard]], with his eye brimful of [[Tears\|tears]],<br>Then checked and rated by Northumberland,<br>Did speak these words, now proved a prophecy?—<br>‘Northumberland, thou ladder by the which<br>My cousin Bolingbroke ascends my throne’—<br>Though then, God knows, I had no such intent,<br>But that necessity so bowed the state<br>That I and [[Greatness\|greatness]] were compelled to kiss—<br>‘The [[Time\|time]] shall come’—thus did he follow it—<br>‘The [[Time\|time]] will come that foul sin, gathering head,<br>Shall break into corruption’; so went on,<br>Foretelling this same [[Time\|time]]’s condition,<br>And the division of our amity. |
| WARWICK | There is a [[history]] in all men’s lives<br>Figuring the natures of the times deceased;<br>The which observed, a man may prophesy,<br>With a near aim, of the main chance of things<br>As yet not come to life, who in their seeds<br>And weak beginnings lie intreasurèd. 80<br>Such things become the hatch and brood of [[Time\|time]];<br>And by the necessary form of this<br>[[King]] [[Richard]] might create a perfect guess<br>That great Northumberland, then false to him,<br>Would of that seed grow to a greater falseness,<br>Which should not find a ground to root upon<br>Unless on you. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Are these things then necessities?<br>Then let us meet them like necessities;<br>And that same word even now cries out on us.<br>They say the Bishop and Northumberland<br>Are fifty thousand strong. |
| WARWICK | It cannot be, my lord.<br>Rumour doth [[Double\|double]], like the voice and echo,<br>The numbers of the feared. Please it your grace<br>To go to bed? Upon my [[Soul\|soul]], my lord,<br>The powers that you already have sent forth<br>Shall bring this prize in very easily.<br>To comfort you the more, I have received<br>A certain instance that Glyndwr is dead.<br>Your majesty hath been this fortnight ill,<br>And these unseasoned hours perforce must add<br>Unto your sickness. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | I will take your counsel.<br>And were these inward wars once out of hand,<br>We would, dear lords, unto the Holy Land. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Justice Shallow\|justice Shallow]] and Justice [[Silence]] |
| SHALLOW | Come on, come on, come on! Give me your hand, sir, give me your hand, sir. An early stirrer, by the rood! And how doth my good cousin [[Silence]]? |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Good morrow, good cousin Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | And how doth my cousin your bedfellow? And your fairest daughter and mine, my god-daughter Ellen? |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Alas, a black ouzel, cousin Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | By yea and no, sir, I dare say my cousin William is become a good scholar. He is at [[Oxford]] still, is he not? 10 |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Indeed, sir, to my cost. |
| SHALLOW | A must then to the Inns o’ [[Court]] shortly. I was once of Clement’s Inn, where I think they will talk of mad Shallow yet. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | You were called ’lusty Shallow’ then, cousin. 15 |
| SHALLOW | By the mass, I was called anything; and I would have done anything indeed, too, and roundly, too. There was I, and little John Doit of Staffordshire, and black George Barnes, and Francis Pickbone, and Will Squeal, a Cotswold man; you had not four such swingebucklers in all the Inns o’ [[Court]] again. And I may say to you, we knew where the bona-robas were, and had the best of them all at commandment. Then was Jack [[Falstaff]], now Sir John, a boy, and page to Thomas [[Mowbray]], Duke of Norfolk. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | This Sir John, cousin, that comes hither anon about soldiers? |
| SHALLOW | The same Sir John, the very same. I see him break Scoggin’s head at the [[Court\|court]] gate when a was a crack, not thus high. And the very same day did I fight with one Samson Stockfish, a fruiterer, behind Gray’s Inn. Jesu, Jesu, the mad days that I have spent! And to see how many of my old acquaintance are dead. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | We shall all follow, cousin. |
| SHALLOW | Certain, ’tis certain; very sure, very sure. [[Death]], as the Psalmist saith, is certain to all; all shall die. How a good yoke of bullocks at Stamford fair? |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | By my troth, I was not there. |
| SHALLOW | [[Death]] is certain. Is old [[Double]] of your town living yet? |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Dead, sir. |
| SHALLOW | Jesu, Jesu, dead! A drew a good bow; and dead! A shot a fine shoot. John o’ Gaunt loved him [[Well\|well]], and betted much money on his head. Dead! A would have clapped i’th’ clout at twelve score, and carried you a forehand shaft a fourteen and fourteen and a half, that it would have done a man’s heart good to see. How a score of ewes now? |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Thereafter as they be. A score of good ewes may be worth ten pounds. |
| SHALLOW | And is old [[Double]] dead? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph and the Page |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Here come two of Sir John [[Falstaff]]’s men, as I think. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW Good morrow, honest gentlemen. |
| BARDOLPH | I beseech you, which is [[Justice Shallow]]? |
| SHALLOW | I am Robert Shallow, sir, a poor esquire of this county, and one of the [[King]]’s Justices of the Peace. What is your good pleasure with me? |
| BARDOLPH | My captain, sir, commends him to you—my captain Sir John [[Falstaff]], a tall gentleman, by heaven, and a most gallant leader. |
| SHALLOW | He greets me [[Well\|well]], sir. I knew him a good backsword man. How doth the good knight? May I ask how my lady his wife doth? |
| BARDOLPH | Sir, pardon, a soldier is better accommodated than with a wife. |
| SHALLOW | It is [[Well\|well]] said, in [[Faith\|faith]], sir, and it is [[Well\|well]] said indeed, too. ‘Better accommodated’—it is good; yea, indeed is it. Good phrases are surely, and ever were, very commendable. ‘Accommodated’—it comes of ‘accommodo’. Very good, a good phrase. |
| BARDOLPH | Pardon, sir, I have heard the word—‘phrase’ call you it?—By this day, I know not the phrase; but I will maintain the word with my [[Sword\|sword]] to be a soldier-like word, and a word of exceeding good command, by heaven. ‘Accommodated’; that is, when a man is, as they say, accommodated; or when a man is being whereby a may be thought to be accommodated; which is an excellent thing. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]] |
| SHALLOW | It is very just. Look, here comes good Sir John. (To Sir John) Give me your hand, give me your worship’s good hand. By my troth, you like [[Well\|well]], and bear your years very [[Well\|well]]. Welcome, good Sir John. |
| SIR JOHN | I am glad to see you [[Well\|well]], good Master Robert Shallow. (To [[Silence]]) Master Surecard, as I think. 85 |
| SHALLOW | No, Sir John, it is my cousin [[Silence]], in commission with me. |
| SIR JOHN | Good Master [[Silence]], it [[Well\|well]] befits you should be of the peace. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Your good worship is welcome. |
| SIR JOHN | Fie, this is hot weather, gentlemen. Have you provided me here half a dozen sufficient men? |
| SHALLOW | Marry, have we, sir. Will you sit? |
| SIR JOHN | Let me see them, I beseech you. |
| *(didascalia)* | He sits |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW Where’s the roll, where’s the roll, where’s the roll? Let me see, let me see, let me see; so, so, so, so, so. Yea, marry, sir: ‘Ralph Mouldy’. To [[Silence]] Let them appear as I call, let them do so, let them do so. Let me see, (calls) where is Mouldy? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Mouldy |
| MOULDY | Here, an’t please you. 100 |
| SHALLOW | What think you, Sir John? A good-limbed fellow, young, strong, and of good friends. |
| SIR JOHN | Is thy name Mouldy? |
| MOULDY | Yea, an’t please you. |
| SIR JOHN | ’Tis the more [[Time\|time]] thou wert used. 105 |
| SHALLOW | Ha, ha, ha, most excellent, i’[[Faith\|faith]]! Things that are mouldy lack use. Very singular good, in [[Faith\|faith]], [[Well\|well]] said, Sir John, very [[Well\|well]] said. |
| SIR JOHN | Prick him. |
| MOULDY | I was pricked [[Well\|well]] enough before, an you could have let me [[Alone\|alone]]. My old dame will be undone now for one to do her husbandry and her drudgery. You need not to have pricked me; there are other men fitter to go out than 1. 114 |
| SIR JOHN | Go to, peace, Mouldy. You shall go, Mouldy; it is [[Time\|time]] you were spent. |
| MOULDY | Spent? |
| SHALLOW | Peace, fellow, peace. Stand aside; know you where you are? 119 |
| *(didascalia)* | Mouldy stands aside |
| SHALLOW | For th‘other, Sir John, let me see: ‘Simon [[Shadow]]’— |
| SIR JOHN | Yea, marry, let me have him to sit under. He’s like to be a cold soldier. |
| SHALLOW | (calls) Where’s [[Shadow]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Shadow]] |
| [[Shadow\|SHADOW]] | Here, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | [[Shadow]], whose son art thou? 125 |
| [[Shadow\|SHADOW]] | My mother’s son, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Thy mother’s son! Like enough, and thy father’s [[Shadow\|shadow]]. So the son of the female is the [[Shadow\|shadow]] of the male—it is often so indeed—but not of the father’s substance. |
| SHALLOW | Do you like him, Sir John? |
| SIR JOHN | [[Shadow]] will serve for [[Summer\|summer]]. Prick him, for we have a number of shadows fill up the muster book. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Shadow]] stands aside |
| SHALLOW | (calls) ‘Thomas Wart.’ |
| SIR JOHN | Where’s he? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Wart |
| WART | Here, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Is thy name Wart? |
| WART | Yea, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Thou art a very ragged wart. |
| SHALLOW | Shall I prick him, Sir John? |
| SIR JOHN | It were superfluous, for his apparel is built upon his back, and the whole frame stands upon pins. Prick him no more. |
| SHALLOW | Ha, ha, ha, you can do it, sir, you can do it! I commend you [[Well\|well]]. 145 |
| *(didascalia)* | Wart stands aside |
| SHALLOW | (Calls) ‘Francis Feeble.’ |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Feeble |
| FEEBLE | Here, sir. |
| SHALLOW | What trade art thou, Feeble? |
| FEEBLE | A woman’s tailor, sir. |
| SHALLOW | Shall I prick him, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | You may, but if he had been a man’s tailor, he’d ha’ pricked you. (To Feeble) Wilt thou make as many holes in an enemy’s battle as thou hast done in a woman’s petticoat? |
| FEEBLE | I will do my good will, sir; you can have no more. |
| SIR JOHN | [[Well]] said, good woman’s tailor; [[Well\|well]] said, courageous Feeble! Thou wilt be as valiant as the wrathful dove or most magnanimous mouse. Prick the woman’s tailor. [[Well]], Master Shallow; deep, Master Shallow. 160 |
| FEEBLE | I would Wart might have gone, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | I would thou wert a man’s tailor, that thou mightst mend him and make him fit to go. I cannot put him to a private soldier that is the leader of so many thousands. Let that suffice, most forcible Feeble. |
| FEEBLE | It shall suffice, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | I am bound to thee, reverend Feeble. |
| *(didascalia)* | Feeble stands aside |
| SIR JOHN | Who is next? |
| SHALLOW | (calls) ‘Peter Bullcalf o’th’ green.’ |
| SIR JOHN | Yea, marry, let’s see Bullcalf. 170 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bullcalf |
| BULLCALF | Here, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Fore God, a likely fellow! Come, prick Bullcalf till he roar again. |
| BULLCALF | O Lord, good my lord captain! |
| SIR JOHN | What, dost thou roar before thou’rt pricked? |
| BULLCALF | O Lord, sir, I am a diseased man. |
| SIR JOHN | What disease hast thou? |
| BULLCALF | A whoreson cold, sir; a cough, sir, which I caught with ringing in the [[King]]’s affairs upon his coronation day, sir. 180 |
| SIR JOHN | Come, thou shalt go to the wars in a gown. We will have away thy cold, and I will take such order that thy friends shall ring for thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Bullcalf stands aside |
| SIR JOHN | Is here all? 184 |
| SHALLOW | There is two more called than your number. You must have but four here, sir, and so I pray you go in with me to dinner. |
| SIR JOHN | Come, I will go drink with you, but I cannot tarry dinner. I am glad to see you, by my troth, Master Shallow. 190 |
| SHALLOW | O, Sir John, do you remember since we lay all [[Night\|night]] in the Windmill in Saint George’s Field? |
| SIR JOHN | No more of that, good Master Shallow, no more of that. |
| SHALLOW | Ha, ’twas a merry [[Night\|night]]! And is Jane Nightwork alive? 196 |
| SIR JOHN | She lives, Master Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | She never could away with me. |
| SIR JOHN | Never, never. She would always say she could not abide Master Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | By the mass, I could anger her to th’ heart. She was then a bona-roba. Doth she hold her own [[Well\|well]]? |
| SIR JOHN | Old, old, Master Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | Nay, she must be old; she cannot choose but be old; certain she’s old; and had Robin Nightwork by old Nightwork before I came to Clement’s Inn. 206 |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | That’s fifty-five year ago. |
| SHALLOW | Ha, cousin [[Silence]], that thou hadst seen that that this knight and I have seen! Ha, Sir John, said I [[Well\|well]]? 210 |
| SIR JOHN | We have heard the chimes at midnight, Master Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | That we have, that we have; in [[Faith\|faith]], Sir John, we have. Our watchword was ‘Hem boys!’ Come, let’s to dinner; come, let’s to dinner. Jesus, the days that we have seen! Come, come. 216 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Shallow, [[Silence]], and Sir John |
| *(didascalia)* | BULLCALF coming forward Good Master Corporate Bardolph, stand my friend, and here’s four Harry ten shillings in French crowns for you. In very [[Truth\|truth]], sir, I had as lief be hanged, sir, as go. And yet for mine own part, sir, I do not care; but rather because I am unwilling, and, for mine own part, have a [[Desire\|desire]] to stay with my friends. Else, sir, I did not care, for mine own part, so much. |
| *(didascalia)* | BARDOLPH taking the money Go to; stand aside. 225 |
| *(didascalia)* | Bullcalf stands aside |
| *(didascalia)* | MOULDY coming forward And, good Master Corporal Captain, for my old dame’s sake stand my friend. She has nobody to do anything about her when I am gone, and she is old and cannot help herself. You shall have forty, sir. 230 |
| BARDOLPH | Go to; stand aside. |
| *(didascalia)* | Mouldy stands aside |
| FEEBLE | By my troth, I care not. A man can die but once. We owe God a [[Death\|death]]. I’ll ne’er bear a base mind. An’t be my destiny, so; an’t be not, so. No man’s too good to serve’s prince. And let it go which way it will, he that dies this year is quit for the next. |
| BARDOLPH | [[Well]] said; thou’rt a good fellow. |
| FEEBLE | [[Faith]], I’ll bear no base mind. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]], Shallow, and [[Silence]] |
| SIR JOHN | Come, sir, which men shall I have? |
| SHALLOW | Four of which you please. |
| BARDOLPH | (to Sir John) Sir, a word with you. (Aside to him)<br>I have three pound to free Mouldy and Bullcalf. |
| SIR JOHN | Go to, [[Well\|well]]. |
| SHALLOW | Come, Sir John, which four will you have? |
| SIR JOHN | Do you choose for me. |
| SHALLOW | Marry, then: Mouldy, Bullcalf, Feeble, and [[Shadow]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Mouldy and Bullcalf. For you, Mouldy, stay at [[Home\|home]] till you are past service; and for your part, Bullcalf, grow till you come unto it. I will none of you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Bullcalf and Mouldy |
| SHALLOW | Sir John, Sir John, do not yourself wrong. They are your likeliest men, and I would have you served with the best. |
| SIR JOHN | Will you tell me, Master Shallow, how to choose a man? Care I for the limb, the thews, the stature, bulk, and big assemblance of a man? Give me the spirit, Master Shallow. Here’s Wart; you see what a ragged appearance it is? A shall charge you and discharge you with the motion of a pewterer’s hammer, come off and on swifter than he that gibbets on the brewer’s bucket. And this same half-faced fellow [[Shadow]]; give me this man. He presents no mark to the enemy; the foeman may with as great aim level at the edge of a penknife. And for a retreat, how swiftly will this Feeble the woman’s tailor run off! O, give me the spare men, and spare me the great ones.—Put me a caliver into Wart’s hand, Bardolph. |
| BARDOLPH | (giving Wart a caliver) Hold, Wart. Traverse—thas, thas, thas! 269 |
| *(didascalia)* | Wart marches |
| SIR JOHN | (to Wart) Come, manage me your caliver. So; very [[Well\|well]]. Go to, very good, exceeding good. O, give me always a little, lean, old, chapped, bald shot! [[Well]] said, i‘[[Faith\|faith]], Wart; thou’rt a good scab. Hold; (giving a coin) there’s a tester for thee. |
| SHALLOW | He is not his craft’s master; he doth not do it right. I remember at Mile-End Green, when I lay at Clement’s Inn—I was then Sir Dagonet in [[arthur\|Arthur]]’s show—there was a little quiver fellow, and a would manage you his piece thus, and a would about and about, and come you in and come you in. ‘Ra-ta-ta!’ would a say; ‘Bounce!’ would a say; and away again would a go; and again would a come. I shall ne’er see such a fellow. |
| SIR JOHN | These fellows will do [[Well\|well]], Master Shallow. God keep you, Master [[Silence]]; I will not use many words with you. Fare you [[Well\|well]], gentlemen both; I thank you. I must a dozen mile tonight.—Bardolph, give the soldiers coats. |
| SHALLOW | Sir John, the Lord bless you; God prosper your affairs! God send us peace! As you return, visit my [[house]]; let our old acquaintance be renewed. Peradventure I will with ye to the [[Court\|court]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Fore God, would you would! |
| SHALLOW | Go to, I have spoke at a word. God keep you! |
| SIR JOHN | Fare you [[Well\|well]], gentle gentlemen. 295 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Shallow and [[Silence]] |
| SIR JOHN | On, Bardolph, lead the men away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Bardolph, Wart, [[Shadow]], and Feeble |
| SIR JOHN | As I return, I will fetch off these justices. I do see the [[Bottom\|bottom]] of [[Justice Shallow]]. Lord, Lord, how subject we old men are to this vice of lying! This same starved justice hath done nothing but prate to me of the wildness of his youth and the feats he hath done about Turnbull Street; and every third word a lie, duer paid to the hearer than the Turk’s tribute. I do remember him at Clement’s Inn, like a man made after supper of a [[cheese]] paring. When a was naked, he was for all the world like a forked radish, with a head fantastically carved upon it with a knife. A was so forlorn that his dimensions, to any thick sight, were invisible. A was the very genius of famine. And now is this Vice’s dagger become a squire, and talks as familiarly of John o’ Gaunt as if he had been sworn brother to him, and I’ll be sworn a ne’er saw him but once, in the Tilt-yard, and then he burst his head for crowding among the marshal’s men. I saw it, and told John o’ Gaunt he beat his own name; for you might have trussed him and all his apparel into an eel-skin. The case of a treble hautboy was a mansion for him, a [[Court\|court]]. And now has he land and beeves. [[Well]], I’ll be acquainted with him if I return; and’t shall go hard but I’ll make him a philosopher’s two stones to me. If the young dace be a bait for the old pike, I see no reason in the law of [[Nature\|nature]] but I may snap at him. Let [[Time\|time]] shape, and there an end. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter in arms the Archbishop of York, Thomas [[Mowbray]], Lord [[Hastings]], and Coleville, within the Forest of Gaultres |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | What is this forest called? |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | ’Tis Gaultres Forest, an’t shall please your grace. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Here stand, my lords, and send discoverers forth<br>To know the numbers of our enemies. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | We have sent forth already. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | ’Tis [[Well\|well]] done.<br>My friends and brethren in these great affairs,<br>I must acquaint you that I have received<br>New-dated letters from Northumberland,<br>Their cold intent, tenor, and substance, thus:<br>Here doth he wish his person, with such powers<br>As might hold sortance with his quality,<br>The which he could not levy; whereupon<br>He is retired to ripe his growing fortunes<br>To Scotland, and concludes in hearty prayers<br>That your attempts may overlive the hazard 15<br>And fearful meeting of their opposite. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Thus do the hopes we have in him touch ground<br>And dash themselves to pieces. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Now, what news? |
| MESSENGER | West of this forest, scarcely off a mile,<br>In goodly form comes on the enemy;<br>And, by the ground they hide, I judge their number<br>Upon or near the rate of thirty thousand. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | The just proportion that we gave them out.<br>Let us sway on, and face them in the field. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl of Westmorland |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | What [[Well\|well]]-appointed leader fronts us here? |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | I think it is my lord of Westmorland. |
| WESTMORLAND | Health and fair greeting from our general,<br>The Prince, Lord John and Duke of Lancaster. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Say on, my lord of Westmorland, in peace,<br>What doth concern your coming. |
| WESTMORLAND | Then, my lord,<br>Unto your grace do I in chief address<br>The substance of my speech. If that rebellion<br>Came like itself, in base and abject routs,<br>Led on by bloody youth, guarded with rags,<br>And countenanced by boys and beggary;<br>I say, if damned commotion so appeared<br>In his true native and most proper shape,<br>You, reverend father, and these noble lords<br>Had not been here to dress the ugly form<br>Of base and bloody insurrection<br>With your fair honours. You, Lord Archbishop,<br>Whose see is by a civil peace maintained,<br>Whose beard the silver hand of peace hath touched,<br>Whose learning and good letters peace hath tutored,<br>Whose white investments figure [[innocence]],<br>The dove and very blessed spirit of peace,<br>Wherefore do you so ill translate yourself<br>Out of the speech of peace that bears such grace<br>Into the harsh and boist’rous tongue of war,<br>Turning your books to graves, your ink to [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Your pens to lances, and your tongue divine<br>To a loud trumpet and a point of war? |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Wherefore do I this? So the question stands.<br>Briefly, to this end: we are all diseased,<br>And with our surfeiting and wanton hours<br>Have brought ourselves into a burning fever,<br>And we must bleed for it—of which disease<br>Our late [[King]] [[Richard]], being infected, died.<br>But, my most noble lord of Westmorland,<br>I take not on me here as a physician,<br>Nor do I as an enemy to peace<br>Troop in the throngs of military men;<br>But rather show a while like fearful war<br>To diet rank minds, sick of happiness,<br>And purge th’obstructions which begin to stop<br>Our very veins of life. Hear me more plainly.<br>I have in equal balance justly weighed<br>What wrongs our arms may do, what wrongs we suffer,<br>And find our griefs heavier than our offences.<br>We see which way the stream of [[Time\|time]] doth run,<br>And are enforced from our most quiet shore<br>By the rough torrent of occasion;<br>And have the summary of all our griefs,<br>When [[Time\|time]] shall serve, to show in articles,<br>Which long ere this we offered to the [[King]],<br>And might by no suit gain our audience.<br>When we are wronged, and would unfold our griefs,<br>We are denied access unto his person<br>Even by those men that most have done us wrong.<br>The dangers of the days but newly gone, 80<br>Whose [[Memory\|memory]] is written on the earth<br>With yet appearing [[Blood\|blood]], and the examples<br>Of every minute’s instance, present now,<br>Hath put us in these ill-beseeming arms,<br>Not to break peace, or any branch of it,<br>But to establish here a peace indeed,<br>Concurring both in name and quality. |
| WESTMORLAND | Whenever yet was your appeal denied?<br>Wherein have you been gallèd by the [[King]]?<br>What peer hath been suborned to grate on you,<br>That you should seal this lawless bloody book<br>Of forged rebellion with a seal divine? |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | My brother general, the commonwealth<br>I make my quarrel in particular. |
| WESTMORLAND | There is no need of any such redress;<br>Or if there were, it not belongs to you. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Why not to him in part, and to us all<br>That feel the bruises of the days before,<br>And suffer the condition of these times<br>To lay a heavy and unequal hand<br>Upon our honours? |
| WESTMORLAND | O my good Lord [[Mowbray]],<br>Construe the times to their necessities,<br>And you shall say indeed it is the [[Time\|time]],<br>And not the [[King]], that doth you injuries.<br>Yet for your part, it not appears to me, 105<br>Either from the [[King]] or in the present [[Time\|time]],<br>That you should have an inch of any ground<br>To build a grief on. Were you not restored<br>To all the Duke of Norfolk’s signories,<br>Your noble and right [[Well\|well]]-remembered father’s? 110 |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | What thing in [[Honour\|honour]] had my father lost<br>That need to be revived and breathed in me?<br>The [[King]] that loved him, as the state stood then,<br>Was force perforce compelled to banish him;<br>And then that [[Henry Bolingbroke]] and he, 115<br>Being mounted and both roused in their seats,<br>Their neighing coursers daring of the spur,<br>Their armed staves in charge, their beavers down,<br>Their eyes of [[Fire\|fire]] sparkling through sights of steel,<br>And the loud trumpet blowing them together,<br>Then, then, when there was nothing could have stayed<br>My father from the breast of Bolingbroke—<br>O, when the [[King]] did throw his warder down,<br>His own life hung upon the staff he threw;<br>Then threw he down himself and all their lives 125<br>That by indictment and by dint of [[Sword\|sword]]<br>Have since miscarried under Bolingbroke. |
| WESTMORLAND | You speak, Lord [[Mowbray]], now you know not what.<br>The Earl of Hereford was reputed then<br>In England the most valiant gentleman.<br>Who knows on whom fortune would then have<br>smiled?<br>But if your father had been victor there,<br>He ne’er had borne it out of Coventry;<br>For all the country in a general voice<br>Cried hate upon him, and all their prayers and [[Love\|love]]<br>Were set on Hereford, whom they doted on<br>And blessed and graced, indeed, more than the [[King]].<br>But this is mere digression from my purpose.<br>Here come I from our princely general<br>To know your griefs, to tell you from his grace<br>That he will give you audience; and wherein<br>It shall appear that your demands are just,<br>You shall enjoy them, everything set off<br>That might so much as think you enemies. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | But he hath forced us to compel this offer,<br>And it proceeds from policy, not [[Love\|love]]. |
| WESTMORLAND | [[Mowbray]], you overween to take it so.<br>This offer comes from [[Mercy\|mercy]], not from [[Fear\|fear]];<br>For lo, within a ken our army lies,<br>Upon mine [[Honour\|honour]], all too confident<br>To give admittance to a thought of [[Fear\|fear]].<br>Our battle is more full of names than yours,<br>Our men more perfect in the use of arms,<br>Our armour all as strong, our cause the best.<br>Then reason will our hearts should be as good.<br>Say you not then our offer is compelled. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | [[Well]], by my will we shall admit no parley. |
| WESTMORLAND | That argues but the shame of your offence.<br>A rotten case abides no handling. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Hath the [[Prince John]] a full commission,<br>In very ample virtue of his father,<br>To hear and absolutely to determine<br>Of what conditions we shall stand upon? |
| WESTMORLAND | That is intended in the general’s name.<br>I muse you make so slight a question. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Then take, my lord of Westmorland, this schedule;<br>For this contains our general grievances.<br>Each several article herein redressed,<br>All members of our cause, both here and hence,<br>That are ensinewed to this action<br>Acquitted by a true substantial form,<br>And present execution of our wills<br>To us and to our purposes consigned,<br>We come within our awe-full banks again,<br>And knit our powers to the arm of peace. |
| WESTMORLAND | (taking the schedule)<br>This will I show the general. Please you, lords,<br>In sight of both our battles we may meet,<br>And either end in peace—which God so frame—<br>Or to the place of diff’rence call the swords<br>Which must decide it. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | My lord, we will do so. 180 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Westmorland |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | There is a thing within my bosom tells me<br>That no conditions of our peace can stand. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | [[Fear]] you not that. If we can make our peace<br>Upon such large terms and so absolute<br>As our conditions shall consist upon,<br>Our peace shall stand as firm as rocky [[Mountains\|mountains]]. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Yea, but our valuation shall be such<br>That every slight and false-derivèd cause,<br>Yea, every idle, nice, and wanton reason,<br>Shall to the [[King]] taste of this action,<br>That, were our royal faiths martyrs in [[Love\|love]],<br>We shall be winnowed with so rough a [[Wind\|wind]]<br>That even our corn shall seem as light as chaff,<br>And good from bad find no partition. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | No, no, my lord; note this. The [[King]] is weary<br>Of dainty and such picking grievances,<br>For he hath found to end one [[Doubt\|doubt]] by [[Death\|death]]<br>Revives two greater in the heirs of life;<br>And therefore will he wipe his tables clean,<br>And keep no tell-tale to his [[Memory\|memory]]<br>That may repeat and [[history]] his loss<br>To new remembrance; for full [[Well\|well]] he knows<br>He cannot so precisely weed this land<br>As his misdoubts present occasion.<br>His foes are so enrooted with his friends<br>That, plucking to unfix an enemy,<br>He doth unfasten so and shake a friend;<br>So that this land, like an offensive wife<br>That hath enraged him on to offer strokes,<br>As he is striking, holds his infant up, 210<br>And hangs resolved correction in the arm<br>That was upreared to execution. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | Besides, the [[King]] hath wasted all his rods<br>On late offenders, that he now doth lack<br>The very instruments of chastisement;<br>So that his power, like to a fangless [[Lion\|lion]],<br>May offer, but not hold. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | ’Tis very true.<br>And therefore be assured, my good Lord Marshal,<br>If we do now make our atonement [[Well\|well]],<br>Our peace will, like a broken limb united,<br>Grow stronger for the breaking. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Be it so. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Westmorland |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Here is returned my lord of Westmorland. |
| WESTMORLAND | The Prince is here at hand. Pleaseth your lordship<br>To meet his grace just distance ’tween our armies? |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Your grace of York, in God’s name then set forward. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Before, and greet his grace!—My lord, we come. |
| *(didascalia)* | They march over the stage. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Prince John]] with one or more soldiers |
| *(didascalia)* | carrying [[Wind\|wind]] |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | You are [[Well\|well]] encountered here, my cousin [[Mowbray]].<br>Good day to you, gentle lord Archbishop;<br>And so to you, Lord [[Hastings]], and to all.<br>My lord of York, it better showed with you<br>When that your flock, assembled by the bell,<br>Encircled you to hear with reverence<br>Your exposition on the holy text,<br>Than now to see you here an iron man,<br>Cheering a rout of rebels with your drum,<br>Turning the word to [[Sword\|sword]], and life to [[Death\|death]].<br>That man that sits within a monarch’s heart<br>And ripens in the sunshine of his favour,<br>Would he abuse the countenance of the [[King]],<br>Alack, what mischiefs might he set abroach<br>In [[Shadow\|shadow]] of such [[Greatness\|greatness]]! With you, Lord Bishop,<br>It is even so. Who hath not heard it spoken<br>How deep you were within the books of God—<br>To us, the speaker in his parliament,<br>To us, th‘imagined voice of God himself,é<br>The very opener and intelligencer<br>Between the grace, the sanctities of heaven<br>And our dull workings? O, who shall believe<br>But you misuse the reverence of your place,<br>Employ the countenance and grace of heav’n<br>As a false favourite doth his prince’s name<br>In deeds dishonourable? You have ta’en up,<br>Under the counterfeited zeal of God,<br>The subjects of his substitute, my father;<br>And, both against the peace of heaven and him,<br>Have here upswarmèd them. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Good my lord of Lancaster,<br>I am not here against your father’s peace;<br>But, as I told my lord of Westmorland,<br>The [[Time\|time]] misordered doth, in common sense,<br>Crowd us and crush us to this monstrous form, 260<br>To hold our safety up. I sent your grace<br>The parcels and particulars of our grief,<br>The which hath been with scorn shoved from the<br>[[Court\|court]],<br>Whereon this Hydra son of war is born;<br>Whose dangerous eyes may [[Well\|well]] be charmed asleep<br>With grant of our most just and right desires,<br>And true obedience, of this [[Madness\|madness]] cured,<br>Stoop tamely to the foot of majesty. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | If not, we ready are to try our fortunes<br>To the last man. |
| [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] | And though we here fall down,<br>We have supplies to second our attempt.<br>If they miscarry, theirs shall second them;<br>And so [[success]] of mischief shall be born,<br>And heir from heir shall hold this quarrel up,<br>Whiles England shall have generation. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | You are too shallow, [[Hastings]], much too shallow,<br>To sound the [[Bottom\|bottom]] of the after-times. |
| WESTMORLAND | Pleaseth your grace to answer them directly<br>How far forth you do like their articles? |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | I like them all, and do allow them [[Well\|well]], 280<br>And swear here, by the [[Honour\|honour]] of my [[Blood\|blood]],<br>My father’s purposes have been mistook,<br>And some about him have too lavishly<br>Wrested his meaning and authority.<br>(To the Archbishop)<br>My lord, these griefs shall be with [[Speed\|speed]] redressed;<br>Upon my [[Soul\|soul]] they shall. If this may please you,<br>Discharge your powers unto their several counties,<br>As we will ours; and here between the armies<br>Let’s drink together friendly and embrace,<br>That all their eyes may bear those tokens [[Home\|home]]<br>Of our restored [[Love\|love]] and amity. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | I take your princely word for these redresses. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | I give it you, and will maintain my word;<br>And thereupon I drink unto your grace.<br>He drinks |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] to Coleville |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Go, captain, and deliver to the army<br>This news of peace. Let them have pay, and part.<br>I know it will [[Well\|well]] please them. Hie thee, captain. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Coleville |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | To you, my noble lord of Westmorland!<br>He drinks |
| WESTMORLAND | (drinking)<br>I pledge your grace. An if you knew what pains<br>I have bestowed to breed this present peace,<br>You would drink freely; but my [[Love\|love]] to ye<br>Shall show itself more openly hereafter. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | I do not [[Doubt\|doubt]] you. |
| WESTMORLAND | I am glad of it.<br>(Drinking) Health to my lord and gentle cousin [[Mowbray]]! |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | You wish me health in very happy season,<br>For I am on the sudden something ill. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Against ill chances men are ever merry;<br>But heaviness foreruns the good event. |
| WESTMORLAND | Therefore be merry, coz, since sudden sorrow<br>Serves to say thus: some good thing comes tomorrow. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Believe me, I am passing light in spirit. 311 |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | So much the worse, if your own rule be true. |
| *(didascalia)* | Shout within |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | The word of peace is rendered. Hark how they shout. |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | This had been cheerful after victory. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | A peace is of the [[Nature\|nature]] of a conquest,<br>For then both parties nobly are subdued,<br>And neither party loser. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | (to Westmorland) Go, my lord,<br>And let our army be discharged too. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Westmorland |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | (To the Archbishop) And, good my lord, so please you,<br>let our trains<br>March by us, that we may peruse the men<br>We should have coped withal. |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Go, good Lord [[Hastings]],<br>And ere they be dismissed, let them march by. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Hastings]] |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | I trust, lords, we shall lie tonight together. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl of Westmorland, with captains |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Now, cousin, wherefore stands our army still? |
| WESTMORLAND | The leaders, having charge from you to stand,<br>Will not go off until they hear you speak. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | They know their duties. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord [[Hastings]] |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Hastings\|HASTINGS]] to the Archbishop Our army is dispersed. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Like youthful steers unyoked, they take their courses,<br>East, west, north, south; or, like a school broke up,<br>Each hurries toward his [[Home\|home]] and sporting place. |
| WESTMORLAND | Good tidings, my lord [[Hastings]], for the which<br>I do arrest thee, traitor, of high treason;<br>And you, Lord Archbishop, and you, Lord [[Mowbray]],<br>Of capital treason I attach you both. |
| *(didascalia)* | The captains guard [[Hastings]], the Archbishop, and [[Mowbray]] |
| [[Mowbray\|MOWBRAY]] | Is this proceeding just and honourable? |
| WESTMORLAND | Is your assembly so? |
| ARCHBISHOP OF YORK | Will you thus break your [[Faith\|faith]]? |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | I pawned thee none.<br>I promised you redress of these same grievances<br>Whereof you did complain; which, by mine [[Honour\|honour]],<br>I will perform with a most Christian care.<br>But for you rebels, look to taste the due<br>Meet for rebellion and such acts as yours.<br>Most shallowly did you these arms commence,<br>Fondly brought here, and foolishly sent hence.—345<br>Strike up our drums, pursue the scattered stray.<br>God, and not we, hath safely fought today.<br>Some guard these traitors to the block of [[Death\|death]],<br>Treason’s true bed and yielder up of breath. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions. Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]] and Coleville |
| SIR JOHN | What’s your name, sir, of what condition are you, and of what place, I pray? |
| COLEVILLE | I am a knight, sir, and my name is Coleville of the Dale. |
| SIR JOHN | [[Well]] then, Coleville is your name, a knight is your degree, and your place the Dale. Coleville shall be still your name, a traitor your degree, and the dungeon your place—a place deep enough, so shall you be still Coleville of the Dale. |
| COLEVILLE | Are not you Sir John [[Falstaff]]? 10 |
| SIR JOHN | As good a man as he, sir, whoe’er I am. Do ye yield, sir, or shall I sweat for you? If I do sweat, they are the drops of thy [[lovers]], and they weep for thy [[Death\|death]]; therefore rouse up [[Fear\|fear]] and trembling, and do observance to my [[Mercy\|mercy]]. 15 |
| COLEVILLE | (kneeling) I think you are Sir John [[Falstaff]], and in that thought yield me. |
| SIR JOHN | (aside) I have a whole school of tongues in this belly of mine, and not a tongue of them all speaks any other word but my name. An I had but a belly of any indifferency, I were simply the most active fellow in Europe. My womb, my womb, my womb undoes me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Prince John]], the Earl of Westmorland, Sir John Blunt, and other lords and soldiers |
| SIR JOHN | Here comes our general. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | The heat is past; follow no further now.<br>A retreat is sounded<br>Call in the powers, good cousin Westmorland. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Westmorland |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Now, [[Falstaff]], where have you been all this while?<br>When everything is ended, then you come.<br>These tardy tricks of yours will, on my life,<br>One [[Time\|time]] or other break some gallows’ back. |
| SIR JOHN | I would be sorry, my lord, but it should be thus. I never knew yet but rebuke and check was the reward of valour. Do you think me a [[Swallow\|swallow]], an arrow, or a bullet? Have I in my poor and old motion the expedition of thought? I have speeded hither with the very extremest inch of possibility; I have foundered nine-score and odd posts; and here, travel-tainted as I am, have in my pure and immaculate valour taken Sir John Coleville of the Dale, a most furious knight and valorous enemy. But what of that? He saw me, and yielded, that I may justly say, with the hook-nosed fellow of Rome, ‘I came, saw, and overcame.’ |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | It was more of his courtesy than your deserving. |
| SIR JOHN | I know not. Here he is, and here I yield him; and I beseech your grace, let it be booked with the rest of this day’s deeds; or, by the Lord, I will have it in a particular ballad else, with mine own picture on the top on‘t, Coleville kissing my foot; to the which course if I be enforced, if you do not all show like gilt twopences to me, and I in the clear sky of fame o’ershine you as much as the full [[Moon\|moon]] doth the cinders of the element, which show like pins’ heads to her, believe not the word of the noble. Therefore let me have right, and let [[Desert\|desert]] mount. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Thine’s too heavy to mount. |
| SIR JOHN | Let it shine then. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Thine’s too thick to shine. |
| SIR JOHN | Let it do something, my good lord, that may do me good, and call it what you will. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Is thy name Coleville? |
| COLEVILLE | It is, my lord. 60 |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | A famous [[Rebel\|rebel]] art thou, Coleville. |
| SIR JOHN | And a famous true subject took him. |
| COLEVILLE | I am, my lord, but as my betters are<br>That led me hither. Had they been ruled by me,<br>You should have won them dearer than you have. |
| SIR JOHN | I know not how—they sold themselves, but thou<br>Like a kind fellow gav’st thyself away,<br>And I thank thee for thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl of Westmorland |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Have you left pursuit? |
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat is made, and execution stayed. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Send Coleville with his confederates 70<br>To York, to present execution.<br>Blunt, lead him hence, and see you guard him sure. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Blunt, with Coleville |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | And now dispatch we toward the [[Court\|court]], my lords.<br>I hear the [[King]] my father is sore sick.<br>(To Westmorland) Our news shall go before us to his<br>majesty,<br>Which, cousin, you shall bear to comfort him;<br>And we with sober [[Speed\|speed]] will follow you. |
| SIR JOHN | My lord, I beseech you give me leave to go<br>Through Gloucestershire, and when you come to [[Court\|court]]<br>Stand, my good lord, pray, in your good report. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Fare you [[Well\|well]], [[Falstaff]]. I in my condition<br>Shall better speak of you than you deserve. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Sir John |
| SIR JOHN | I would you had but the wit; ‘twere better than your dukedom. Good [[Faith\|faith]], this same young sober-blooded boy doth not [[Love\|love]] me, nor a man cannot make him laugh. But that’s no marvel; he drinks no wine. There’s never none of these demure boys come to any proof; for thin drink doth so overcool their [[Blood\|blood]], and making many fish meals, that they fall into a kind of male green-sickness; and then when they marry, they get wenches. They are generally fools and cowards—which some of us should be too, but for inflammation. A good sherry-sack hath a two-fold operation in it. It ascends me into the brain, dries me there all the foolish and dull and crudy vapours which environ it, makes it apprehensive, quick, forgetive, full of nimble, fiery, and delectable shapes, which, delivered o’er to the voice, the tongue, which is the birth, becomes excellent wit. The second property of your excellent sherry is the warming of the [[Blood\|blood]], which, before cold and settled, left the liver white and pale, which is the badge of pusillanimity and cowardice. But the sherry warms it, and makes it course from the inwards to the parts’ extremes; it illuminateth the face, which, as a beacon, gives warning to all the rest of this little kingdom, man, to arm; and then the vital commoners and inland petty spirits muster me all to their captain, the heart; who, great and puffed up with his retinue, doth any deed of [[Courage\|courage]]. And this valour comes of sherry. So that skill in the weapon is nothing without sack, for that sets it a-work; and learning a mere hoard of gold kept by a devil, till sack commences it and sets it in act and use. Hereof comes it that Prince Harry is valiant; for the cold [[Blood\|blood]] he did naturally inherit of his father he hath, like lean, sterile, and bare land, manured, husbanded, and tilled, with excellent endeavour of drinking good, and good store of fertile sherry, that he is become very hot and valiant. If I had a thousand sons, the first human principle I would teach them should be to forswear thin potations, and to addict themselves to sack. 121 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph |
| SIR JOHN | How now, Bardolph? |
| BARDOLPH | The army is discharged all and gone. |
| SIR JOHN | Let them go. I’ll through Gloucestershire, and there will I visit Master Robert Shallow, Esquire. I have him already tempering between my finger and my thumb, and shortly will I seal with him. Come, away! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Henry in his bed, attended by the [[Earl of Warwick]], Thomas Duke of [[Clarence]], Humphrey [[Duke of Gloucester]], and others |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Now, lords, if God doth give successful end<br>To this [[debate]] that bleedeth at our doors,<br>We will our youth lead on to higher fields,<br>And draw no swords but what are sanctified.<br>Our navy is addressed, our power collected,<br>Our substitutes in absence [[Well\|well]] invested,<br>And everything lies level to our wish;<br>Only we want a little personal strength,<br>And pause us till these rebels now afoot<br>Come underneath the yoke of [[government]]. 10 |
| WARWICK | Both which we [[Doubt\|doubt]] not but your majesty<br>Shall soon enjoy. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Humphrey, my son of [[Gloucester]],<br>Where is the Prince your brother? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I think he’s gone to hunt, my lord, at Windsor. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And how accompanied? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I do not know, my lord. 15 |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Is not his brother Thomas of [[Clarence]] with him? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | No, my good lord, he is in presence here. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | What would my lord and father? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Nothing but [[Well\|well]] to thee, Thomas of [[Clarence]].<br>How chance thou art not with the Prince thy brother?<br>He loves thee, and thou dost neglect him, Thomas.<br>Thou hast a better place in his affection<br>Than all thy brothers. Cherish it, my boy,<br>And noble offices thou mayst effect<br>Of mediation, after I am dead, 25<br>Between his [[Greatness\|greatness]] and thy other brethren.<br>Therefore omit him not, blunt not his [[Love\|love]],<br>Nor lose the good advantage of his grace<br>By seeming cold or careless of his will;<br>For he is gracious, if he be observed; 30<br>He hath a tear for pity, and a hand<br>Open as day for melting [[Charity\|charity]].<br>Yet notwithstanding, being incensed, he is flint,<br>As humorous as [[Winter\|winter]], and as sudden<br>As flaws congealed in the [[Spring\|spring]] of day.<br>His temper therefore must be [[Well\|well]] observed.<br>Chide him for faults, and do it reverently,<br>When you perceive his [[Blood\|blood]] inclined to mirth;<br>But being moody, give him line and scope<br>Till that his passions, like a whale on ground,<br>Confound themselves with working. Learn this,<br>Thomas,<br>And thou shalt prove a shelter to thy friends,<br>A hoop of gold to bind thy brothers in,<br>That the united vessel of their [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Mingled with venom of suggestion—<br>As force perforce the age will pour it in-<br>Shall never leak, though it do work as strong<br>As aconitum or rash gunpowder. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | I shall observe him with all care and [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why art thou not at Windsor with him, Thomas? 50 |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | He is not there today; he dines in [[London]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And how accompanied? Canst thou tell that? |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | With Poins and other his continual followers. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Most subject is the fattest soil to weeds,<br>And he, the noble image of my youth,<br>Is overspread with them; therefore my grief<br>Stretches itself beyond the hour of [[Death\|death]].<br>The [[Blood\|blood]] weeps from my heart when I do shape<br>In forms imaginary th’unguided days<br>And rotten times that you shall look upon 60<br>When I am sleeping with my ancestors;<br>For when his headstrong riot hath no curb,<br>When rage and hot [[Blood\|blood]] are his counsellors,<br>When means and lavish manners meet together,<br>O, with what wings shall his affections fly<br>Towards fronting peril and opposed [[Decay\|decay]]? |
| WARWICK | My gracious lord, you look beyond him quite.<br>The Prince but studies his companions,<br>Like a strange tongue, wherein, to gain the language,<br>’Tis needful that the most immodest word<br>Be looked upon and learnt, which once attained,<br>Your highness knows, comes to no further use<br>But to be known and hated; so, like gross terms,<br>The Prince will in the perfectness of [[Time\|time]]<br>Cast off his followers, and their [[Memory\|memory]]<br>Shall as a pattern or a measure live<br>By which his grace must mete the lives of other,<br>Turning past evils to advantages. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | ’Tis seldom when the bee doth leave her comb<br>In the dead carrion. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl of Westmorland |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Who’s here? Westmorland? 80 |
| WESTMORLAND | Health to my sovereign, and new happiness<br>Added to that that I am to deliver I<br>[[Prince John]] your son doth kiss your grace’s hand.<br>[[Mowbray]], the Bishop Scrope, [[Hastings]], and all<br>Are brought to the correction of your law.<br>There is not now a [[Rebel\|rebel]]’s [[Sword\|sword]] unsheathed,<br>But peace puts forth her olive everywhere.<br>The manner how this action hath been borne<br>Here at more leisure may your highness read,<br>With every course in his particular.<br>He gives the [[King]] papers |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O Westmorland, thou art a [[Summer\|summer]] bird<br>Which ever in the haunch of [[Winter\|winter]] sings<br>The lifting up of day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Harcourt |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Look, here’s more news. |
| HARCOURT | From enemies heaven keep your majesty;<br>And when they stand against you, may they fall<br>As those that I am come to tell you of!<br>The Earl Northumberland and the Lord Bardolph,<br>With a great power of English and of Scots,<br>Are by the sheriff of Yorkshire overthrown.<br>The manner and true order of the fight<br>This packet, please it you, contains at large.<br>He gives the [[King]] papers |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And wherefore should these good news make me sick?<br>Will fortune never come with both hands full,<br>But write her fair words still in foulest letters?<br>She either gives a stomach and no food—<br>Such are the poor in health—or else a feast,<br>And takes away the stomach—such are the rich,<br>That have abundance and enjoy it not.<br>I should rejoice now at this happy news,<br>And now my sight fails, and my brain is giddy. 110<br>O me! Come near me now; I am much ill.<br>He swoons |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Comfort, your majesty! |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | O my royal father! |
| WESTMORLAND | My sovereign lord, cheer up yourself, look up. |
| WARWICK | Be patient, princes; you do know these fits<br>Are with his highness very ordinary. 115<br>Stand from him, give him air; he’ll straight be [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | No, no, he cannot long hold out these pangs.<br>Th’incessant care and labour of his mind<br>Hath wrought the mure that should confine it in<br>So thin that life looks through and will break out. 120 |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | The people [[Fear\|fear]] me, for they do observe<br>Unfathered heirs and loathly births of [[Nature\|nature]].<br>The [[Seasons\|seasons]] change their manners, as the year<br>Had found some months asleep and leaped them over. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | The [[River\|river]] hath thrice flowed, no ebb between, 125<br>And the old folk, [[Time\|time]]’s doting chronicles,<br>Say it did so a little [[Time\|time]] before<br>That our great grandsire Edward sicked and died. |
| WARWICK | Speak lower, princes, for the [[King]] recovers. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | This apoplexy will certain be his end. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | I pray you take me up and bear me hence<br>Into some other chamber; softly, pray. |
| *(didascalia)* | The [[King]] is carried over the stage in his bed |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Let there be no noise made, my gentle friends,<br>Unless some dull and favourable hand<br>Will whisper music to my weary spirit. |
| WARWICK | Call for the music in the other room. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more. Still music within |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Set me the [[Crown\|crown]] upon my pillow here. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Clarence]] takes the [[Crown\|crown]] from the [[King]]’s head, and sets it on his pillow |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | His eye is hollow, and he changes much. |
| *(didascalia)* | A noise within |
| WARWICK | Less noise, less noise! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Prince Harry |
| PRINCE HARRY | Who saw the Duke of [[Clarence]]? |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | I am here, brother, full of heaviness. |
| PRINCE HARRY | How now, rain within doors, and none abroad?<br>How doth the [[King]]? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Exceeding ill. |
| PRINCE HARRY | Heard he the good news yet? Tell it him. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | He altered much upon the hearing it. |
| PRINCE HARRY | If he be sick with joy, he’ll recover without physic. WARWICK<br>Not so much noise, my lords! Sweet prince, speak low.<br>The [[King]] your father is disposed to [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Let us withdraw into the other room. |
| WARWICK | Will’t please your grace to go along with us? |
| PRINCE HARRY | No, I will sit and watch here by the [[King]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but the [[King]] and Prince Harry |
| PRINCE HARRY | Why doth the [[Crown\|crown]] lie there upon his pillow,<br>Being so troublesome a bedfellow?<br>O polished perturbation, golden care,<br>That keep‘st the ports of slumber open wide<br>To many a watchful [[Night\|night]]!—[[Sleep]] with it now;<br>Yet not so sound, and half so deeply sweet,<br>As he whose brow with homely biggen bound<br>Snores out the watch of [[Night\|night]]. O majesty,<br>When thou dost pinch thy bearer, thou dost sit<br>Like a rich armour worn in heat of day,<br>That scald’st with safety.—By his gates of breath<br>There lies a downy feather which stirs not.<br>Did he suspire, that light and weightless down<br>Perforce must move.—My gracious lord, my father!—<br>This [[Sleep\|sleep]] is sound indeed. This is a [[Sleep\|sleep]]<br>That from this golden rigol hath divorced<br>So many English kings.—Thy due from me<br>Is [[Tears\|tears]] and heavy sorrows of the [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Which [[Nature\|nature]], [[Love\|love]], and filial tenderness<br>Shall, O dear father, pay thee plenteously.<br>My due from thee is this imperial [[Crown\|crown]],<br>Which, as immediate from thy place and [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Derives itself to me.<br>He puts the [[Crown\|crown]] on his head<br>Lo where it sits,<br>Which God shall guard; and put the world’s whole<br>strength<br>Into one giant arm, it shall not force<br>This lineal [[Honour\|honour]] from me. This from thee<br>Will I to mine leave, as ’tis left to me. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Music ceases. The [[King]] awakes |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Warwick, [[Gloucester]], [[Clarence]]! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]], and the Dukes of |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | [[Gloucester]] and [[Clarence]] |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | Doth the [[King]] call? |
| WARWICK | What would your majesty? How fares your grace? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why did you leave me here [[Alone\|alone]], my lords? |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | We left the Prince my brother here, my liege,<br>Who undertook to sit and watch by you. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | The Prince of Wales? Where is he? Let me see him. |
| WARWICK | This door is open; he is gone this way. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | He came not through the chamber where we stayed. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Where is the [[Crown\|crown]]? Who took it from my pillow? |
| WARWICK | When we withdrew, my liege, we left it here. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | The Prince hath ta’en it hence. Go seek him out.<br>Is he so hasty that he doth suppose<br>My [[Sleep\|sleep]] my [[Death\|death]]?<br>Find him, my lord of Warwick; chide him hither. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Warwick |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | This part of his conjoins with my disease,<br>And helps to end me. See, sons, what things you are,<br>How quickly [[Nature\|nature]] falls into revolt<br>When gold becomes her object!<br>For this the foolish over-careful fathers<br>Have broke their [[Sleep\|sleep]] with thoughts, their brains with<br>care,<br>Their bones with industry; for this they have<br>Engrossed and piled up the cankered heaps<br>Of strange-achieved gold; for this they have<br>Been thoughtful to invest their sons with arts<br>And martial exercises; when, like the bee<br>Culling from every flower the virtuous sweets,<br>Our thighs packed with wax, our mouths with honey,<br>We bring it to the hive; and, like the [[Bees\|bees]], 206<br>Are murdered for our pains. This bitter taste<br>Yields his engrossments to the ending father. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Now where is he that will not stay so long<br>Till his friend sickness have determined me? |
| WARWICK | My lord, I found the Prince in the next room,<br>Washing with kindly [[Tears\|tears]] his gentle cheeks<br>With such a deep demeanour, in great sorrow,<br>That tyranny, which never quaffed but [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Would, by beholding him, have washed his knife 215<br>With gentle eye-drops. He is coming hither. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | But wherefore did he take away the [[Crown\|crown]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Prince Harry with the [[Crown\|crown]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Lo where he comes.—Come hither to me, Harry.<br>(To the others) Depart the chamber; leave us here<br>[[Alone\|alone]]. Exeunt all but the [[King]] and Prince Harry |
| PRINCE HARRY | I never thought to hear you speak again. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Thy wish was father, Harry, to that thought.<br>I stay too long by thee, I weary thee.<br>Dost thou so hunger for mine empty chair<br>That thou wilt needs invest thee with my honours<br>Before thy hour be ripe? O foolish youth,<br>Thou seek‘st the [[Greatness\|greatness]] that will overwhelm thee!<br>Stay but a little, for my cloud of dignity<br>Is held from falling with so weak a [[Wind\|wind]]<br>That it will quickly drop. My day is dim.<br>Thou hast stol’n that which after some few hours<br>Were thine without offence, and at my [[Death\|death]]<br>Thou hast sealed up my expectation.<br>Thy life did manifest thou loved’st me not,<br>And thou wilt have me die assured of it.<br>Thou hid’st a thousand daggers in thy thoughts,<br>Whom thou hast whetted on thy stony heart<br>To stab at half an hour of my life.<br>What, canst thou not forbear me half an hour?<br>Then get thee gone and dig my [[Grave\|grave]] thyself,<br>And bid the merry [[Bells\|bells]] ring to thine ear<br>That thou art crowned, not that I am dead.<br>Let all the [[Tears\|tears]] that should bedew my hearse<br>Be drops of balm to sanctify thy head.<br>Only compound me with forgotten [[Dust\|dust]].<br>Give that which gave thee life unto the worms.<br>Pluck down my officers, break my decrees;<br>For now a [[Time\|time]] is come to mock at form—<br>Harry the Fifth is crowned. Up, [[Vanity\|vanity]]!<br>Down, royal state! All you sage counsellors, hence!<br>And to the English [[Court\|court]] assemble now<br>From every region, apes of idleness!<br>Now, neighbour confines, purge you of your scum I<br>Have you a ruffian that will swear, drink, [[Dance\|dance]],<br>Revel the [[Night\|night]], rob, murder, and commit<br>The oldest sins the newest kind of ways?<br>Be happy; he will trouble you no more.<br>England shall [[Double\|double]] gild his treble [[Guilt\|guilt]],<br>England shall give him office, [[Honour\|honour]], might;<br>For the fifth Harry from curbed licence plucks<br>The muzzle of restraint, and the wild dog 260<br>Shall flesh his tooth on every [[Innocent\|innocent]].<br>O my poor kingdom, sick with civil blows!<br>When that my care could not withhold thy riots,<br>What wilt thou do when riot is thy care?<br>O, thou wilt be a wilderness again,<br>Peopled with wolves, thy old inhabitants. |
| PRINCE HARRY | O pardon me, my liege! But for my [[Tears\|tears]],<br>The moist impediments unto my speech,<br>I had forestalled this dear and deep rebuke<br>Ere you with grief had spoke and I had heard<br>The course of it so far. There is your [[Crown\|crown]]; |
| *(didascalia)* | He returns the [[Crown\|crown]] and kneels |
| PRINCE HARRY | And He that wears the [[Crown\|crown]] immortally<br>Long guard it yours! If I affect it more<br>Than as your [[Honour\|honour]] and as your renown,<br>Let me no more from this obedience rise,<br>Which my most true and inward duteous spirit<br>Teacheth this prostrate and exterior bending.<br>God witness with me, when I here came in<br>And found no course of breath within your majesty,<br>How cold it struck my heart. If I do feign,<br>O, let me in my present wildness die,<br>And never live to show th‘incredulous world<br>The noble change that I have purposed.<br>Coming to look on you, thinking you dead,<br>And dead almost, my liege, to think you were,<br>I spake unto this [[Crown\|crown]] as having sense,<br>And thus upbraided it: ‘The care on thee depending<br>Hath fed upon the [[Body\|body]] of my father;<br>Therefore thou best of gold art worst of gold.<br>Other, less fine in carat, is more precious,<br>Preserving life in medicine potable;<br>But thou, most fine, most honoured, most renowned,<br>Hast eat thy bearer up.’ Thus, my royal liege,<br>Accusing it, I put it on my head,<br>To try with it, as with an enemy<br>That had before my face murdered my father,<br>The quarrel of a true inheritor.<br>But if it did infect my [[Blood\|blood]] with joy<br>Or swell my thoughts to any strain of [[Pride\|pride]],<br>If any [[Rebel\|rebel]] or vain spirit of mine<br>Did with the least affection of a welcome<br>Give entertainment to the might of it,<br>Let God for ever keep it from my head,<br>And make me as the poorest vassal is,<br>That doth with awe and terror kneel to it. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O my son,<br>God put it in thy mind to take it hence,<br>That thou mightst win the more thy father’s [[Love\|love]],<br>Pleading so wisely in excuse of it!<br>Come hither, Harry; sit thou by my bed,<br>And hear, I think, the very latest counsel<br>That ever I shall breathe. |
| *(didascalia)* | Prince Harry rises from kneeling and sits by the bed |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | God knows, my son,<br>By what bypaths and indirect crook’d ways<br>I met this [[Crown\|crown]]; and I myself know [[Well\|well]]<br>How troublesome it sat upon my head.<br>To thee it shall descend with better quiet,<br>Better opinion, better confirmation;<br>For all the soil of the achievement goes<br>With me into the earth. It seemed in me<br>But as an [[Honour\|honour]] snatched with boist‘rous hand;<br>And I had many living to upbraid<br>My gain of it by their assistances,<br>Which daily grew to quarrel and to bloodshed,<br>Wounding supposed peace. All these bold fears<br>Thou seest with peril I have answerèd;<br>For all my reign hath been but as a scene<br>Acting that argument. And now my [[Death\|death]]<br>Changes the mood, for what in me was purchased<br>Falls upon thee in a more fairer sort,<br>So thou the garland wear’st successively.<br>Yet though thou stand‘st more sure than I could do,<br>Thou art not firm enough, since griefs are green,<br>And all thy friends—which thou must make thy<br>friends—<br>Have but their stings and teeth newly ta’en out,<br>By whose fell working I was first advanced,<br>And by whose power I [[Well\|well]] might lodge a [[Fear\|fear]]<br>To be again displaced; which to avoid<br>I cut them off, and had a purpose now<br>To lead out many to the Holy Land,<br>Lest rest and lying still might make them look<br>Too near unto my state. Therefore, my Harry,<br>Be it thy course to busy giddy minds<br>With foreign quarrels, that action hence borne out<br>May waste the [[Memory\|memory]] of the former days.<br>More would I, but my lungs are wasted so<br>That strength of speech is utterly denied me.<br>How I came by the [[Crown\|crown]], O God forgive,<br>And grant it may with thee in true peace live! |
| PRINCE HARRY | My gracious liege,<br>You won it, wore it, kept it, gave it me;<br>Then plain and right must my possession be,<br>Which I with more than with a common [[Pain\|pain]]<br>’Gainst all the world will rightfully maintain. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Prince John]] of Lancaster followed by the [[Earl of Warwick]] and others |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Look, look, here comes my John of Lancaster. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Health, peace, and happiness to my royal father! |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Thou bring’st me happiness and peace, son John;<br>But health, alack, with youthful wings is flown<br>From this bare withered trunk. Upon thy sight<br>My worldly business makes a period.<br>Where is my lord of Warwick? |
| PRINCE HARRY | My lord of Warwick! |
| *(didascalia)* | Warwick comes forward to the [[King]] |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Doth any name particular belong<br>Unto the lodging where I first did swoon? |
| WARWICK | ’Tis called Jerusalem, my noble lord. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Laud be to God! Even there my life must end.<br>It hath been prophesied to me many years<br>I should not die but in Jerusalem,<br>Which vainly I supposed the Holy Land;<br>But bear me to that chamber; there I’ll lie;<br>In that Jerusalem shall Harry die. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt, bearing the [[King]] in his bed |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Shallow, [[Silence]], Sir John [[Falstaff]], Bardolph, and the Page |
| SHALLOW | (to Sir John ) By cock and pie, you shall not away tonight.—What, Davy, I say! |
| SIR JOHN | You must excuse me, Master Robert Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | I will not excuse you; you shall not be excused; excuses shall not be admitted; there is no excuse shall serve; you shall not be excused.—Why, Davy! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Davy |
| DAVY | Here, sir. |
| SHALLOW | Davy, Davy, Davy; let me see, Davy; let me see. William Cook—bid him come hither.—Sir John, you shall not be excused. |
| DAVY | Marry, sir, thus: those precepts cannot be served. And again, sir: shall we sow the headland with wheat? |
| SHALLOW | With red wheat, Davy. But for William Cook; are there no young pigeons? |
| DAVY | Yes, sir. Here is now the [[smith]]’s note for shoeing and plough-irons. |
| SHALLOW | Let it be cast and paid. Sir John, you shall not be excused. |
| DAVY | Sir, a new link to the bucket must needs be had; and, sir, do you mean to stop any of William’s wages, about the sack he lost at Hinkley Fair? |
| SHALLOW | A shall answer it. Some pigeons, Davy, a couple of short-legged hens, a joint of mutton, and any pretty little tiny kickshaws, tell William Cook. |
| DAVY | Doth the man of war stay all [[Night\|night]], sir? |
| SHALLOW | Yea, Davy. I will use him [[Well\|well]]; a friend i’th’ [[Court\|court]] is better than a penny in purse. Use his men [[Well\|well]], Davy, for they are arrant knaves, and will backbite. |
| DAVY | No worse than they are back-bitten, sir, for they have marvellous foul linen. |
| SHALLOW | [[Well]] conceited, Davy. About thy business, Davy. |
| DAVY | I beseech you, sir, to countenance William Visor of Wo’ncot against Clement Perks o’th’ [[Hill]]. |
| SHALLOW | There is many complaints, Davy, against that Visor. That Visor is an arrant knave, on my knowledge. |
| DAVY | I grant your worship that he is a knave, sir; but yet God forbid, sir, but a knave should have some countenance at his friend’s request. An honest man, sir, is able to speak for himself, when a knave is not. I have served your worship truly, sir, this eight years. An I cannot once or twice in a quarter bear out a knave against an honest man, I have little credit with your worship. The knave is mine honest friend, sir; therefore I beseech you let him be countenanced. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW Go to; I say he shall have no wrong. Look about, Davy. Exit Davy Where are you, Sir John? Come, off with your boots.—Give me your hand, Master Bardolph. |
| BARDOLPH | I am glad to see your worship. 49 |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW I thank thee with all my heart, kind Master Bardolph. To the Page And welcome, my tall fellow.—Come, Sir John. |
| SIR JOHN | I’ll follow you, good Master Robert Shallow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Shallow With [[Silence]] |
| SIR JOHN | Bardolph, look to our horses. 54 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Bardolph With the Page |
| SIR JOHN | If I were sawed into quantities, I should make four dozen of such bearded hermits’ staves as Master Shallow. It is a wonderful thing to see the semblable coherence of his men’s spirits and his. They, by observing him, do bear themselves like foolish justices; he, by conversing with them, is turned into a justice-like servingman. Their spirits are so married in conjunction, with the participation of society, that they flock together in consent like so many wild geese. If I had a suit to Master Shallow, I would [[Humour\|humour]] his men with the imputation of being near their master; if to his men, I would curry with Master Shallow that no man could better command his servants. It is certain that either wise bearing or ignorant carriage is caught as men take diseases, one of another; therefore let men take heed of their company. I will devise matter enough out of this Shallow to keep Prince Harry in continual laughter the wearing out of six fashions—which is four terms, or two actions—and a shall laugh without intervallums. O, it is much that a lie with a slight oath, and a jest with a sad brow, will do with a fellow that never had the ache in his shoulders! O, you shall see him laugh till his face be like a wet cloak ill laid up! |
| SHALLOW | (within) Sir John! |
| SIR JOHN | I come, Master Shallow; I come, Master Shallow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Warwick]] at one door, and the [[Lord Chief Justice]] at another door |
| WARWICK | How now, my [[Lord Chief Justice]], whither away ? |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | How doth the [[King]] ? |
| WARWICK | Exceeding [[Well\|well]]: his cares are now all ended. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I [[Hope\|hope]] not dead. |
| WARWICK | He’s walked the way of [[Nature\|nature]],<br>And to our purposes he lives no more. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I would his majesty had called me with him.<br>The service that I truly did his life<br>Hath left me open to all injuries. |
| WARWICK | Indeed I think the [[Young King\|young King]] loves you not. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I know he doth not, and do arm myself<br>To welcome the condition of the [[Time\|time]],<br>Which cannot look more hideously upon me<br>Than I have drawn it in my fantasy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Prince John]] of Lancaster, and the Dukes of [[Clarence]] and [[Gloucester]] |
| WARWICK | Here come the heavy issue of dead Harry.<br>O, that the living Harry had the temper<br>Of he the worst of these three gentlemen !<br>How many nobles then should hold their places,<br>That must strike sail to spirits of vile sort ! |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | O God, I [[Fear\|fear]] all will be overturned. |
